in emergency situations.

Appendix 4 provides information in relation to anchoring, stability and boat handling.

Regular testing of communications equipment should be carried out to confirm correct operation.

3.5 Communications

It is recommended that all vessels operating on inland lakes and waterways carry a VHF radio. Handheld VHF batteries should be fully charged prior to departure. Vessel

3.6 Spread of invasive species by boats on inland waters

In recent years a number of new invasive species of flora and fauna,

### 3.6 Spread of invasive species by boats on inland waters

*Pages 102–102*

such as the Zebra Mussel, Asian Clam, Lagarosiphon and knotweeds have been introduced into Irish lakes, rivers and riparian zones (lands along the edges of rivers, streams, lakes). These species impact in a negative manner on existing natural species, in many cases leading to their extinction.

▪Angling equipment, including nets

and waders, should be thoroughly washed, disinfected and dried out before being used on a fishing trip;

▪Anglers should use artificial

lure in place of live bait – check prohibitions on the use of live bait with Inland Fisheries Ireland;

▪Avoid launching boats/engines on

multiple waterways. Use locally hired boats if possible and always ask what biosecurity measures have been put in place by boat hire operators.

Many lakes and waterways now require boats to be registered to operate and visitor boats are controlled. Measures have been put in place, which boat owners should follow, to limit the propagation of new species into our inland waters. These measures include:

Further information is available from the National Parks and Wildlife Service:

▪If travelling to a new area, make

National Parks & Wildlife Service 90 King Street North Dublin 7 D07 N7CV Tel: +353 (0)1 888 2000 E-mail: natureconservation@npws.gov.ie Web: www.npws.ie

prior enquiries regarding any control measures that may be applied by the relevant authorities. For example the National Parks and Wildlife Service should be contacted where boats/water craft are being introduced into a National Park or Nature Reserve, as a licence or permission may be required. In addition, anglers should contact Inland Fisheries Ireland, which has a network of local offices (contact details available at www.fisheriesireland. ie and Appendix 10.3) to seek advice on disinfection facilities for boats, equipment, etc.;

Information regarding the spread of invasive species is also available at www.invasivespeciesireland.com.

Anglers should contact Inland Fisheries Ireland for further angling specific information:

Inland Fisheries Ireland 3044 Lake Drive Citywest Business Campus Dublin 24 D24 CK66

▪Always thoroughly clean/wash

and disinfect your boat, engine and trailer prior to launching, particularly if you are moving between rivers and lakes;

Tel: +353 (0) 1 884 2600 Email: info@fisheriesireland.ie Web: www.fisheriesireland.ie

## Sailing Dinghies

### Sailing Dinghies

*Pages 103–106*

## 4

Sailing Dinghies

Think and Prepare

Checklist of Basic Requirements and Advice

It is recommended that all owners and users of sailing dinghies should follow this Checklist of Basic Requirements and Advice both before going on the water and while on the water:

Get training from an approved training provider in the correct use of the

craft you wish to use;

Wear a Personal Flotation Device/Lifejacket: Know about the legal

requirements in relation to the wearing of Personal Flotation Devices/ Lifejackets and the need for the proper care and maintenance of such devices. These requirements are dealt with in Part A of the Code of Practice. Select the proper type based on activity or boating conditions. See Marine Notice No. 32 of 2019;

Check current weather forecasts and water conditions prior to

departure and plan your voyage accordingly. If bad weather is forecast, consider postponing your trip. Strong tides and currents can carry you away very quickly, so watch what is happening around you to avoid getting caught out. If in doubt, don’t go out! (see Appendix 6, Weather, Sea States and Tides);

Check for any Hazards and Risks and Prepare a Passage Plan - See

Appendix 8 of the Code of Practice for an example of a passage planning template and note the information on hazards and risk assessment on pages 10 – 16. Assess the hazards and risks before leaving shore but continue to be aware during your trip as the conditions can change;

Avoid operating alone: Be aware of the additional risks involved with

single person operation when there is nobody else on board or nearby to help you or to raise the alarm. Make sure you are able to re-board your craft in the event that you fall overboard;

Carry out Pre-Departure Safety Checks and Briefing – Check the

condition of your craft and safety equipment. All persons on board should know what to do in the event of an emergency, where the safety/emergency equipment is on board, e.g. flares, radio equipment, etc., and how to use the equipment;

Think and Prepare

Checklist of Basic Requirements and Advice

Tell someone about the plans for your trip - a designated person ashore

should know your departure and return times, where you are going, and what to do to raise the alarm if necessary. See page 20 for a suggested information template;

Let the Coast Guard know: Before you go out on the water, it is

recommended that all recreational craft users log a Traffic Report message via the nearest Coast Guard Radio Station – see page 18 for further information;

Bring a dependable means of communication. VHF radio equipment is

the recommended method of communication and craft users should be familiar with its use. Mobile phones should not be relied on and should only be considered as a back up means of communication. Where mobile phone use is proposed, the phone should be in a waterproof pouch with a lanyard, be fully charged at all times and the signal strength and charge indicator should be regularly checked while the vessel is underway;

Consider bringing an appropriate Personal Locator Beacon (PLB) or

Emergency Position Indicating Radio Beacon (EPIRB) as these provide an effective means to send a distress alert. See Marine Notice No. 25 of 2010 as amended, Marine Notice No. 38 of 2013, Marine Notice No. 70 of 2022 and sections 1.2.12.1, 11.3 and Appendix 2 of the Code for further information, including in relation to the registration of such devices;

Be responsible – do not be under the influence of alcohol or drugs

during a trip: when operating a recreational craft, a person must not be under the influence of alcohol or drugs or any combination of drugs or of drugs and alcohol to such an extent as to be incapable of having proper control of the craft;

Be aware of and recognise the symptoms of fatigue – get proper sleep

and rest before your journey;

Wear suitable clothing and footwear;

Think and Prepare

Checklist of Basic Requirements and Advice

Float to live: If you unexpectedly fall into the water, the initial cold

shock can cause you to gasp, breathe uncontrollably, breathe in water and panic. If you are not wearing a Personal Flotation Device or lifejacket, lay your head back with ears submerged. Stay calm, try to relax and breath normally. Try to float or tread water while you catch your breath. A properly fitted lifejacket will keep your head above water, ensuring you gasp air. Once the cold shock has passed, try to get hold of something that will help you float and get as much of your body out of the water as possible. To lessen heat escape, keep your legs together with arms and elbows by your side. Once you are calm, call for help;

In a marine emergency, raise the alarm on VHF Channel 16 or call999

or 112 and ask for the Coast Guard.

### 4.3 Pre-Launch Checks prior to entering the water

*Pages 107–107*

4.1 Training

10. Always ensure that a designated person ashore is aware of departure and return times and have a procedure in place to raise the alarm if necessary. Membership of a club will generally offer this facility in addition to providing safety boats for members engaged in club activities.

It is recommended that dinghy sailors undertake appropriate training, including in capsize and recovery techniques. A number of training schemes and approved courses are available and information may be obtained directly from course providers (see Appendix 9 for details of course providers).

11. Check weather and sea conditions prior to departure.

4.2 Safety Recommendations

If you are dinghy sailing, you should adhere to the following practices.

12. Avoid main shipping lanes (see Appendix 3).

1. Always wear a suitable PFD/ lifejacket when sailing. Buoyancy provided by a wet suit or dry suit is not sufficient (see Part A of this Code for statutory requirements).

13. Sail in company for safety.

14. Be aware of your own personal capabilities in handling a boat under various weather conditions.

2. Wear suitable clothes, wetsuits or drysuits, particularly if there is potential to capsize.

15. If your dinghy is operating in a remote area, carry additional safety equipment, i.e. a handheld VHF radio and additional pyrotechnics (parachute rocket red flares and hand-held flares).

3. Your dinghy should have an effective means of bailing water.

4. Carry a paddle on board.

5. Carry a towing painter on board.

4.3 Pre-Launch Checks prior to entering the water

6. The crew in the dinghy should be familiar with capsize recovery techniques and towing techniques.

To protect dinghy operators, shipping and other water users, dinghies may be subject to specific local Bye-Laws issued by harbour or local authorities to control or restrict areas of operation. It is the responsibility of dinghy operators to be familiar with the relevant Rules of Navigation, local Notices to Mariners and Bye- Laws in force in the waters in which they are sailing.

7. Ensure that the dinghy has sufficient buoyancy. Check all buoyancy tanks and plugs prior to departure.

8. Check the hull, fittings, rigging and sails before going afloat.

9. Always be aware of overhead wires when rigging or manoeuvring a dinghy ashore or afloat.

### 5.2 Recommended equipment for personal watercraft

*Pages 113–113*

Personal Watercraft (PWC) such as jet skis or water/wet bikes are a rapidly growing segment of the recreational craft market.

▪A flare pack should be carried

containing a minimum of 1 x pinpoint red, 2 x orange smoke in a watertight container.

▪Carry a suitable folding anchor.

A PWC is a watercraft that is less than 4.88 metres (16 feet) long, propelled by a two stroke petrol engine directly powering a water jet pump and designed to be operated by a person who sits, stands or kneels on it. They are capable of speeds exceeding 100km/hr and are highly manoeuvrable in competent hands.

▪A collapsible paddle, which can

be stowed on board for use in the event of engine failure.

5.1 Training

It is recommended that persons operating a PWC undertake appropriate training. There are a number of training schemes and approved courses available and information can be obtained directly from course providers (see Appendix 9 for details of course providers).

▪Basic first aid kit.

▪PWC should be equipped with

a lanyard/kill cord to cut out the engine; the lanyard/kill cord must be attached to the operator's body, clothing, or PFD/lifejacket. A spare lanyard/kill cord should always be carried on board.

5.2 Recommended equipment for personal watercraft

▪Rope (5m x 8mm Nylon) for use in

towing and mooring.

![Figure from page 113](assets/p113-f01.webp)

*Figure from page 113*

▪Knife.

▪Torch.

▪Basic Tool Kit.

▪Fire Extinguisher.

especially for salt water riding.

All persons on board a jet ski must wear a personal flotation device (PFD) or a lifejacket.

5.4.2 Emergency Procedures

▪In the event of falling off the

craft into cold water, re-board immediately. Immersion in cold water can result in a life-threatening drop in body temperature (hypothermia). Hypothermia can also be caused by wind chill, rain and perspiration. To avoid this danger, prepare by dressing correctly including wetsuit/drysuit, hand/ foot protection. Wearing your PFD/lifejacket affords additional protection against the cold.

5.4 Personal watercraft operations 5.4.1 Prior to entering the water (Pre-Launch Checks)

▪To protect other water users and

due to high noise levels generated, PWC may be subject to specific local bye-laws issued by harbour or local authorities to restrict speed or the areas of operation. It is the responsibility of PWC operators to be familiar with the relevant bye-laws in force in the waters they use.

▪If in difficulty, remain with your

PWC. Do not try to swim to shore in cold water unless you are very close to safety and you have no expectation of speedy assistance. Swimming and treading water use up valuable energy and produce rapid heat loss. If it is not

▪Check the weather/sea condition

forecast before starting out.

▪Always complete a safety

checklist (see section 5.7) prior to departure. A waterproof laminated

towing a float, it is essential to carry an observer. This should only be done on craft with a carrying capacity of 3 or more persons.

▪Always ensure sufficient fuel is on

board for any intended trip. Plan to return with the fuel tank one third full thereby allowing for any possible emergencies.

5.4.3 On the water

▪Do not drink alcohol or take drugs

when riding a PWC: it is illegal to do so and your ability to make quick decisions is impaired. This is critical when operating a fast and manoeuvrable PWC.

▪Additional petrol should not be

carried on board and no fuel transfer should be attempted once the vessel is afloat.

▪Study the manufacturer's manual.

5.5 Offshore Cruising

Practice the handling of your PWC under experienced supervision in open water and well away from other boaters.

Subject to suitable weather and sea conditions, these craft are capable of undertaking coastal or offshore passages.

▪Be alert for the wave, wind, cloud

changes that may signal weather changes.

▪Such passages should be

undertaken in company with other PWCs or suitable support boats.

▪Avoid skiing alone, especially at

sea.

▪Avoid busy swimming areas.

areas that may be defined by a series of marker buoys inside of which all craft are prohibited from entering.

It is recommended that the following are carried on board:

▪A high incidence of accidents

with PWCs involve collisions with other craft. Operators should exercise the utmost caution when approaching or overtaking other boats and should never manoeuvre at speed in close proximity to other PWC, boats or swimmers (see Appendix 1).

▪Chart of sea area.

The majority of PWCs are not fitted with navigation lights, which the law requires for operating at night. Therefore the use of such PWCs is restricted to a period between sunrise and sunset.

5.8 High Speed Power/Sports Boats – Operation and handling

A high-speed power/sports boat is generally regarded as one capable of achieving speeds in excess of 17 knots. There are a variety of craft designed as high speed power/sports boats, including rigid GRP Vee profile hulls, RIBs, Cathedral type dory hulls, etc. The high speeds that can be attained by these craft place specific

### 5.8 High Speed Power/Sports Boats – Operation and handling

*Pages 117–117*

Competency and Skills - Ensure you have received adequate training in the operation and handling characteristics of the boat type you are using.

demands on the skills and capabilities of their operators. These craft allow a much shorter reaction time to an incident than conventional motor craft.

It is recommended that persons participating in power/sports boat activities undertake appropriate training. A number of training schemes and approved courses are available and information can be obtained directly from course providers (see Appendix 9).

▪Check engine oil levels, etc;

▪Ensure all on board wear suitable

clothing. Be aware of the effects of wind chill at speed;

▪Carry sunscreen protection - SPF

30 or higher and UVA protection;

▪If using an inflatable boat or RIB,

ensure all tubes are correctly inflated.

Persons under 16 years of age are not allowed to be in command of a fast power/sports craft, i.e. a craft that can attain a speed through or over water of 17 knots or more.

▪Maintain a good all round lookout,

particularly when in main shipping channels;

▪Observe designated speed limits,

particularly on passage through anchorages, marina approaches and areas used by swimmers;

your ability and capability;

Personal Watercraft – Jet Skis and High Speed Power Boats

The Powerboat Association of Northern Ireland is the National Authority for powerboat racing in Ireland. All competitive activity is run in accordance with the rules and procedures of the Union Internationale Motonautique (UIM), which is the world governing body for Powerboating. The UIM rules set out requirements for vessel safety, skipper qualification, scrutiny requirements and safety management.

▪If intending to swim from your

boat, consider its freeboard. Check beforehand and make sure there is a suitable means to re-board from the water.

5.9 Waterskiing, Wakeboarding and towed rides

Persons involved in recreational towed activities such as waterskiing, wakeboarding and the towing of inflatable doughnuts, etc., should be aware of the following:

5.10.1 Powerboat Race Training

▪A suitable PFD/lifejacket must be

All participants and officials involved in powerboat racing must complete the relevant UIM Training Programme for their role. UIM Training Programmes are structured into three distinct modules – Basic Powerboat Training for those new to the sport, Advanced Training provides an introduction into powerboat racing and Competition Training to provide the skills necessary to compete in powerboat racing at a national and international level.

worn.

▪There should always be an

observer in addition to the boat driver aboard the towing craft.

▪Towing rides should only be

undertaken in areas either specifically designated or in areas clear of other shipping and water users.

▪Ensure your Insurance Company is

aware of these activities.

When waterskiing, both observer and skier should be aware of the correct procedures and signals required. It is recommended that individuals undertake suitable training and coaching.

There is a range of powerboat racing disciplines including Pleasure Navigation to Circuit Powerboat Racing on sheltered or inland stretches of water to Offshore Racing on open waters over set distances. For further information, visit www.powerboatni.org.

The Irish Waterski and Wakeboard Federation is the national body for waterskiing in Ireland (see Appendix 10 for contact details).

carry you offshore or away from your start point.

6.2 Windsurfing Safety 6.2.1 Prior to entering the water

▪Examine your rigging for worn

ropes or loose fittings. Ensure all are correctly cleated and tied off.

▪Check the condition of the board.

you are going and when you will be back.

▪Check the local weather and sea

area forecast for the area you propose to sail in.

### 6.4.1 Safe Surfing Guidelines

*Pages 125–125*

6.4.1 Safe Surfing Guidelines

▪Be aware of hypothermia and

leave the water if symptoms occur (shivering, numb extremities and poor co-ordination).

The following are some Safe Surfing Guidelines from Irish Surfing that apply to various surfing disciplines:

▪Always ensure you are off the

Learn to surf at an Irish Surfing registered surf club or surf school.

water well before sunset.

6.3 National Association

The Irish Windsurfing Association (IWA) is an affiliated club of Irish Sailing and as such is the governing body for competitive windsurfing. Windsurfing events are run in accordance with the safety guidelines of the IWA (see Appendix 10 for contact details).

Do not attempt surfing unless you can swim.

Do not surf alone or enter the water as dusk is approaching or if you have been drinking alcohol.

Always let somebody on land know where you have gone and when you will return.

6.4 Other water based activities

While this Code of Practice is focused primarily on the safe operation of navigable recreational craft, there are many other waterbased activities where appropriate training, knowledge and safety awareness are of similar importance.

Check the weather and tides before you paddle out. Learn to observe the ocean so you can identify rips, wind changes and other hazards.

With the increased popularity of activities such as surfing and kite surfing, attention is drawn to a range of safety information leaflets and guidelines that are available to view or download on the www. safetyonthewater.ie website and from bodies such as Water Safety Ireland at www.watersafety.ie and the Irish Surfing Association at www.irishsurfing.ie.

If you are unfamiliar with a break, check with local surfers or other water users before paddling out. Ensure the waves you are surfing are of a size and power suitable to your ability. Do not get too confident. Stick to beaches until you become experienced.

Make sure your equipment, especially your leash, is in good order. Remember it is easier to spot a brightly coloured surfboard or wetsuit at sea in the event that you require rescuing.

### 6.4.2 Foiling Safety

*Pages 126–126*

6.4.2 Foiling Safety

When you “wipeout”, do not come to the surface too soon and when you do come to the surface protect your head with your arms.

A wide variety of foiling is becoming increasingly popular. This includes wing foiling and electric foils. Like windsurfing and kitesurfing, there are safety concerns, both for those using the foil and for swimmers in the vicinity.

Windsurfing, Foiling and Kitesurfing

Always check behind you for other water users before abandoning your surfboard to dive under a wave.

As with all water-based activities, it is important to get the right training. It is important to be able to swim well and develop an understanding of the water and its dangers.

If you get caught in a rip do not try to paddle against it, paddle across it. If you find yourself in difficulty, stay calm, do not discard your board, wave one arm in the air and shout to attract attention. Do not panic.

Before you go on the water:

▪It is recommended to take a

training course from a qualified instructor.

Always respect the rescue services. Should you see a surfer in difficulty in the water, DON’T DELAY, phone 112 or 999 and ask for the Coast Guard. Even the most experienced surfer may at some time require assistance.

hydrofoil is in good condition.

▪Conduct regular maintenance

of the hydrofoil to ensure safe operation over sustained use.

▪Prepare a plan. Go in pairs/groups

and make sure someone on shore knows your plans.

Be aware of other water users and always show respect to other craft.

▪Factor in water depth and any

underwater hazards due to the hydrofoil under the board.

▪Check the weather conditions

and be aware of rapidly changing weather conditions.

▪Be especially aware of offshore

winds or currents that can carry you towards the open sea.

▪The hydrofoil has sharp edges

which can pose a danger to yourself and others.

Canoeing/Kayaking and Stand-Up Paddle

Boarding

Think and Prepare

Checklist of Basic Requirements and Advice

It is recommended that those taking part in canoeing, kayaking or stand-up paddle boarding activities should follow this Checklist of Basic Requirements and Advice both before going on the water and while on the water:

Get training from an approved training provider in the correct use of the

type of craft you wish to use. Practice capsize and recovery drills;

Wear a suitable Personal Flotation Device/Lifejacket: Know about

the legal requirements in relation to the wearing of Personal Flotation Devices/Lifejackets and the need for the proper care and maintenance of such devices. These requirements are dealt with in Part A of the Code of Practice. Note that the term Personal Flotation Device includes a buoyancy aid;

Use a suitable paddle for your craft. Use a suitable leash when stand-

up paddle boarding;

Check current weather forecasts and water conditions prior to

departure and plan your voyage accordingly (see Appendix 6, Weather, Sea States and Tides);

Check for any Hazards and Risks and Prepare a Passage Plan - See

Appendix 8 of the Code of Practice for an example of a passage planning template and note the information on hazards and risk assessment on pages 10 – 16. Assess the hazards and risks before leaving shore but continue to be aware during your trip as the conditions can change;

Avoid operating alone: Be aware of the additional risks involved with

single person operation when there is nobody else nearby to help you or to raise the alarm;

Be aware of local bye-laws: Avoid restricted areas and busy swimming

areas;

Think and Prepare

Checklist of Basic Requirements and Advice

Carry out Pre-Departure Safety Checks and Briefing – Check the

condition of your craft and safety equipment;

Tell someone about the plans for your trip - a designated person ashore

should know your departure and return times, where you are going, and what to do to raise the alarm if necessary. See page 20 for a suggested information template;

Let the Coast Guard know: Before you go out on the water, it is

recommended that all recreational craft users log a Traffic Report message via the nearest Coast Guard Radio Station – see page 18 for further information;

Bring a dependable means of communication. VHF radio equipment is

the recommended method of communication and craft users should be familiar with its use. Mobile phones should not be relied on and should only be considered as a back up means of communication;

Consider bringing an appropriate Personal Locator Beacon (PLB) as

these provide an effective means to send a distress alert. See section 1.2.12.1 and Appendix 2 of the Code for further information, including in relation to the registration of such devices;

Be responsible – do not be under the influence of alcohol or drugs

during a trip: when operating a recreational craft, a person must not be under the influence of alcohol or drugs or any combination of drugs or of drugs and alcohol to such an extent as to be incapable of having proper control of the craft;

Be aware of and recognise the symptoms of fatigue – get proper sleep

and rest before your journey;

Wear suitable clothing and footwear;

Think and Prepare

Checklist of Basic Requirements and Advice

Float to live: If you unexpectedly fall into the water, the initial cold

shock can cause you to gasp, breathe uncontrollably, breathe in water and panic. If you are not wearing a Personal Flotation Device or lifejacket, lay your head back with ears submerged. Stay calm, try to relax and breath normally. Try to float or tread water while you catch your breath. A properly fitted lifejacket will keep your head above water, ensuring you gasp air. Once the cold shock has passed, try to get hold of something that will help you float and get as much of your body out of the water as possible. To lessen heat escape, keep your legs together with arms and elbows by your side. Once you are calm, call for help;

In a marine emergency, raise the alarm on VHF Channel 16 or call 999

or 112 and ask for the Coast Guard.

A canoe is a lightweight narrow boat, typically pointed at both ends and open on top, propelled by one or more seated or kneeling paddlers facing the direction of travel using a single-bladed paddle.

a life-saving course.

A kayak is a small, narrow boat which is propelled by means of a doublebladed paddle.

▪Never operate alone, always canoe

in company.

▪Do not operate a canoe if under

the influence of alcohol or drugs.

In this Code of Practice, the term canoe when used also refers to a kayak.

▪Inspect your craft and equipment

thoroughly. Check it is fitted with adequate buoyancy material and that such buoyancy is correctly distributed and secured within the hull.

There are a number of basic safety precautions that should be applied to any canoeing activity, regardless of its speciality.

▪Ensure that the bung is fitted

correctly.

Canoeing Ireland have a comprehensive training and accreditation scheme, which covers river, sea kayaking, open canoes and stand-up paddle boarding on inland waterways. 7.4 Sea Kayaking

Kayaking on the sea presents unique challenges and risks compared with other disciplines of kayaking. It is recommended that participants get sea kayak specific training and join a club before participating. Inflatable craft such as inflatable kayaks are not suitable for use in sea kayaking. In addition to the safety advice and requirements in sections 7.1, 7.2 and 7.3, sea kayakers should observe the following precautions:

river, consider carrying some of the following:

▪Be aware of the river’s grading

and of the water level before committing. The different grades are listed in the Table below:

▫Split paddles;

▫Webbing slings and carabiners;

▫Duct tape;

Grade I: Flat Water

Water is stationary or extremely slow moving and without any obstructions.

Grade II: Moderately Difficult

The way down a river is clear but simple obstructions do exist. Small stoppers and small drops can be present. There are places where the flow accelerates. There is a choice of routes.

Grade III: Difficult

There is a route that is easily recognisable from the water. Waves can be irregular. Boulders and obstructions can be numerous. Stoppers and small eddies exist. Inspection is advisable.

Grade IV: Very Difficult

The route is not always clear and inspection is advisable. Rapids are continuous and breakouts are few and small. Stoppers are powerful. Continual manoeuvring with precise control and good decision making is required.

Grade V: Extremely Difficult

Inspection is essential because serious dangers can exist. Large drops, narrow passages, very complex boulder fields, ever changing water and difficult holes are characteristic of this grade. Difficulties are continuous.

The Canoeing Ireland website has more detail on the levels of river grading (https://www.canoe.ie/river-grading-and-area-definitions/).

Rowing Boats

Think and Prepare

Checklist of Basic Requirements and Advice

It is recommended that all owners and users of rowing boats should follow this Checklist of Basic Requirements and Advice both before going on the water and while on the water:

Get training from an approved training provider in the correct use of

the type of craft you wish to use. Be familiar with and instructed in the capsize drill;

Wear a Personal Flotation Device/Lifejacket: Know about the legal

requirements in relation to the wearing and carrying of Personal Flotation Devices/Lifejackets and the need for the proper care and maintenance of such devices. These requirements are dealt with in Part A of the Code of Practice. Select the proper type based on activity or conditions. See Marine Notice No. 32 of 2019;

Check current weather forecasts and water conditions prior to

departure and plan your voyage accordingly (see Appendix 6, Weather, Sea States and Tides);

Check for any Hazards and Risks and Prepare a Passage Plan - See

Appendix 8 of the Code of Practice for an example of a passage planning template and note the information on hazards and risk assessment on pages 10 – 16. Assess the hazards and risks before leaving shore but continue to be aware during your trip as the conditions can change;

Avoid operating alone: Be aware of the additional risks involved with

single person operation when there is nobody else nearby to help you or to raise the alarm;

Carry out Pre-Departure Safety Checks and Briefing – Check the

condition of your craft and safety equipment. All persons on board should know what to do in the event of an emergency, where the safety/emergency equipment is on board, e.g. flares, radio equipment, etc., and how to use the equipment;

Think and Prepare

Checklist of Basic Requirements and Advice

Tell someone about the plans for your trip - a designated person

ashore should know your departure and return times, where you are going, and what to do to raise the alarm if necessary. See page 20 for a suggested information template;

Let the Coast Guard know: Before you go out on the water, it is

recommended that all recreational craft users log a Traffic Report message via the nearest Coast Guard Radio Station – see page 18 for further information;

Bring a dependable means of communication. VHF radio equipment is

the recommended method of communication and craft users should be familiar with its use. Mobile phones should not be relied on and should only be considered as a back up means of communication. Where mobile phone use is proposed, the phone should be in a waterproof pouch with a lanyard, be fully charged at all times and the signal strength and charge indicator should be regularly checked while the vessel is underway;

Consider bringing an appropriate Personal Locator Beacon (PLB) or

Emergency Position Indicating Radio Beacon (EPIRB) as these provide an effective means to send a distress alert. Where a number of boats are on the water at one time, perhaps the attending safety boat could carry a PLB or EPIRB. See sections 1.2.12.1, 11.3 and Appendix 2 of the Code for further information, including in relation to the registration of such devices;

Be responsible – do not be under the influence of alcohol or drugs

during a trip: when operating a recreational craft, a person must not be under the influence of alcohol or drugs or any combination of drugs or of drugs and alcohol to such an extent as to be incapable of having proper control of the craft;

Be aware of and recognise the symptoms of fatigue – get proper sleep

and rest before your journey;

Wear suitable clothing and footwear;

Think and Prepare

Checklist of Basic Requirements and Advice

Float to live: If you unexpectedly fall into the water, the initial cold

shock can cause you to gasp, breathe uncontrollably, breathe in water and panic. If you are not wearing a Personal Flotation Device or lifejacket, lay your head back with ears submerged. Stay calm, try to relax and breath normally. Try to float or tread water while you catch your breath. A properly fitted lifejacket will keep your head above water, ensuring you gasp air. Once the cold shock has passed, try to get hold of something that will help you float and get as much of your body out of the water as possible. As per the capsize drill, remember to stay with your boat. To lessen heat escape, keep your legs together with arms and elbows by your side. Once you are calm, call for help;

In a marine emergency, raise the alarm on VHF Channel 16 or call 999

or 112 and ask for the Coast Guard.

### 8.3 Olympic style rowing boats and safety boats

*Pages 145–145*

Rowing includes “Olympic Style” rowing boats, racing gigs/skiffs and traditional racing currachs.

▪In the event that you enter the

water, where possible stay with the boat, get out of the water by getting back in the boat or on top of the upturned hull in accordance with the capsize drill.

8.1 Training

It is recommended that persons involved in rowing activities should undertake appropriate training. A number of training schemes and approved courses are available and information may be obtained from Rowing Ireland and the Irish Coastal Rowing Federation (see Appendix 9 for details of course providers).

▪Be aware of the dangers of

hypothermia when wet or exposed to the elements.

▪It is important to consider

and establish an effective and dependable means of communication, other than by solely using mobile phone technology, for the specific location of the activities to be undertaken.

All rowers should be familiar with and instructed in the capsize drill during initial introduction training to the sport of Rowing. All rowers should receive refresher training in the capsize drill periodically.

All participants should be aware of the requirements set out in the Rowing Ireland Safety Manual and the Row Safe publication, which are available on the Rowing Ireland website at www.rowingireland.ie.

8.2 Prior to going on the water

▪Ensure you are a competent

swimmer and capable of surviving in the water in the areas you operate in. Rowers should be able to swim at least 50 metres in light clothing.

Exercise care when climbing into and out of boats from pontoons, jetties, quay walls or other landing platforms. Procedures should be practiced and subject to training.

▪It is important to risk assess local

conditions, currents, wind strength and direction, etc., prior to putting craft into the water. Boats should not be operated in waters that are beyond the capabilities of the crew or the boats concerned.

8.3 Olympic style rowing boats and safety boats

These boats are used in the rowing events in the Olympic Games and are governed in Ireland by Rowing Ireland. The following safety points should be adhered to at all times.

▪Prepare for the trip you are

taking. Ensure you are dressed appropriately for the conditions and that your hair, if long, is tied up so that it does not interfere with your rowing and to avoid entanglement.

A coach and/or a safety boat should always be in attendance and other safety precautions must be followed. Operators of safety boats should be

Recreational Craft are classed into 6 categories:

Charter Boats/Bare Boat Hire

Category A - Ocean

Category B - Offshore

Category C - Coastal

Category D - Inshore / Estuary

Category E - Inland waterways including large open lakes

Category F - Inland Waterways - open boats less than 7 m in length operating in rivers and sheltered areas on lakes in moderate weather conditions.

Hire craft in Irish waters are generally confined to Categories C, D, E and F.

### 10.2 Marina Safety

*Pages 156–157*

Chapter 10 provides basic guidance on safety and maintenance.

▪Tenders other than inflatable

types should have additional buoyancy fitted. This may be in the form of dedicated buoyancy tubes, polyethylene foam block or integral buoyancy chambers built into the boat.

10.1 Tendering operations to moored craft

There are instances where boats are moored offshore due to tidal or draft restrictions and access to them is achieved by the use of a smaller tender launched from shore. In such instances, where a tender is used to access and board a moored vessel, the following precautions should be taken:

▪All tenders should, if utilising an

outboard engine, also carry a set of oars or paddles and be fitted with a suitable painter.

▪The tender should not be

overloaded by either personnel or equipment. Many tenders are fitted with a manufacturer’s instruction plate on the transom identifying the maximum number of persons or equipment, or combinations of each, a tender may safely carry and the maximum power of any outboard engine that may be safely fitted to the boat – do not exceed these figures.

▪Crew must wear a PFD/lifejacket

at all times for the operation of boarding the tender, transit to and boarding of the moored craft.

▪It is recommended that operators

carry a waterproof handheld VHF radio.

▪Persons under the influence

of alcohol or drugs should not participate in tendering operations.

▪Suitable means of boarding the

moored vessel should be provided, e.g. boarding ladder, access gates on railings, etc.

▪Ensure the tender is in good

condition and suitably equipped. If using an inflatable dinghy type, check the tubes are correctly inflated and the dinghy is a multitube type capable of remaining afloat in the event of failure of any single tube.

10.2 Marina Safety

While the use of a marina facility offers benefits of increased convenience and comfort to boat operators, it should be noted that a marina is a potentially dangerous location. Many marinas are located in deep water that is subject to strong tidal streams, are exposed to strong winds and are fitted with a minimum of protective barriers. Strong tidal streams can present a danger in the event of an accident.

▪Be aware of tidal and wind

conditions prior to commencing any tendering operation.

▪If launching directly from shore,

ensure that the launch point is a safe departure point, can be safely accessed and is not subject to excessive surf.

crew members in the process of berthing the boat. A short step and not a long jump is the required transit from boat to berth.

Take note of speed limits and "no wake areas" in harbours, approaching marinas or near swimming areas.

Users are encouraged to observe the following precautions when using and moving about on marina installations:

▪Instruct all crew on deck to wear

a PFD/lifejacket when bringing a boat on/off a marina berth.

▪Do not leave children unattended

▪Wear suitable non-slip footwear.

### 10.5.1 Rigging

*Pages 160–160*

10.5 Maintenance 10.5.1 Rigging

▪Always wear a suitable PFD/

lifejacket and ensure all loose gear is correctly secured.

Rigging components on a sailing craft are subject to extreme loadings. If these are set up incorrectly in the beginning and adjusted subsequently, the fluctuations in load can result in fatigue failure of stays, spreaders or masts, despite the use of modern materials.

▪Ensure sufficient experienced crew

are available. Do not attempt to launch short-handed.

▪Advise a responsible person

ashore of your plans, including departure and return times, launch locations and intended destinations. Always inform them of your safe return.

Rigging demands constant attention and inspection on an ongoing basis. While a boat is in service, it must be recognised that despite the use of materials such as stainless steel, components such as shrouds and terminals will not last indefinitely and must be replaced. On many boats greater than 6 metres in length, current practice is not to remove the mast at the end of the season. Consequently, rigs remain in place for many years without proper inspection.

▪Check with the relevant local

authority/local Beach Bye-laws that there is no prohibition on beach launching.

### 10.5.6 Tool Kit

*Pages 163–164*

10.6 Electrical systems in recreational craft

![Figure from page 163](assets/p163-f02.webp)

*Figure from page 163*

Electrical systems on recreational craft can vary from simple 12volt starting batteries to complex high voltage installations incorporating generators, advanced navigation, communication and domestic service systems.

All electrical installations are subject to prolonged exposure to a hostile marine environment, including vibration, moisture and mechanical damage. Furthermore, they can be compromised as they degrade due to aging. Retrofitting of electrical equipment by non-competent individuals also poses serious risk to vessels.

10.5.6 Tool Kit

Craft should carry a suitable and relevant tool kit comprising of the following suggested items:

Failure of electrical components can result in a number of scenarios ranging from loss of essential propulsion machinery, fire, damage to hull fittings due to stray electrical currents and, on occasion, injury or loss of life due to electrocution. It is therefore essential that electrical systems on recreational vessels are routinely inspected and maintained by a competent person.

▪Screwdriver set.

▪Spanner set applicable to each

craft.

▪Adjustable spanner.

▪Torch.

▪Spark plug spanner (in the case of

petrol engines).

▪Junior hacksaw and spare blades.

▪Pliers and vise grips.

▪Can of WD40 release oil, if

1. Carry out regular visual checks of cabling and ensure that all cable runs and connections are secure and free of any mechanical or water damage.

2. Using a competent marine electrician, conduct regular ‘earth checks’ and ‘megger tests’ on cables and, where appropriate, equipment. Earth faults should be cleared promptly, especially on systems having a voltage to earth of greater than 55 V.

6. The routine testing of electrical systems as part of the vessel’s maintenance schedule is very important and should include the following:

▪Earthing/bonding arrangements

3. Pay particular attention to the way that additional equipment is connected, irrespective of whether it is permanently or temporarily installed. The power supply should be taken from a point in the circuit which is protected by a fuse or circuit breaker and never connected directly to the battery or alternator or associated wiring. All such work should be undertaken by a competent electrical contractor.

mechanical damage to cables and securing of cables

If using lead Acid type batteries stored inside a vessel, ensure there is a suitable means of venting the storage space to atmosphere. Provide some ventilation for the space where “sealed” or “maintenance free” type batteries are located. Be aware of the dangers of fire or electrocution due to shorting of battery terminals when working with batteries.

7. Sufficient spares should be carried onboard to enable repair of a vessel’s essential systems in the event of failure.

8. Any failure of critical systems should be investigated immediately to determine the cause, and any repairs should be carried out by a qualified individual.

10.7 Liquified/Liquid Petroleum Gas (LPG)

The following safety devices should be fitted to any LPG system:

Many recreational craft are fitted with Liquid Petroleum Gas (LPG) installations and appliances, primarily in the form of cookers. LPG is supplied in pressurised cylinders and is usually propane, butane or a mixture of the two gases.

▪Manual Isolation valve on the

cylinder;

▪Fixed pressure regulator located

within the storage locker to provide a fixed working pressure to the consuming device;

When handled incorrectly, the dangers associated with LPG systems include fire, explosion, burns and asphyxiation, due to gas leakage from the system or accumulation of gas following flame failure in an appliance. Such incidents have caused loss of life and material damage on recreational craft.

▪A pressure relief device located on

or adjacent to the cylinder;

▪An automatic safety gas cut out

device located on the cylinder or adjacent to it, to cut off the gas supply in the event of a loss of pressure due to a gas leak;

### 10.8 Vessel Refuelling

*Pages 166–166*

Emergency Action In the event of a gas leak being detected, the following actions should be taken:

▪A manual shut-off valve located

adjacent to the appliance, but easily accessed for the purpose of operation;

Operational Safety, including at Marinas, and Maintenance

▪Gas detectors, located in the

▪Isolate the gas supply at the

space appliances are situated. Detectors should be located in bilge areas or at floor level to detect accumulating gas leakage. Alarms should be audible and arranged to automatically cut off the gas supply from the bottle;

bottle;

▪Extinguish all flames and

cigarettes. Do not operate any electrical equipment or switches;

▪Ventilate the space, being aware

that as the gas is heavier than air, it will sink into bilges;

▪Carbon Monoxide alarms and local

fire alarms should also be fitted in spaces containing cookers;

▪Be aware of the location of all fire

extinguishing equipment;

▪All gas safety isolation and safety

▪Do not operate the system until

devices should be clearly marked to indicate their function and the open and closed positions.

the fault has been corrected by a qualified technician.

Further information and advice is contained in Marine Notice No. 37 of 2017 (Use of liquefied petroleum gas (LPG) installations and systems on merchant vessels, fishing vessels, pleasure craft and other marine craft).

Only appliances designed for use in a marine environment should be installed on a vessel. The cooker should be fitted with a gas shut-off. Systems should be subject to annual maintenance by a qualified technician and all onboard alarms and shut-offs should be checked at least monthly when the vessel is in use.

10.8 Vessel Refuelling

Refuelling of boats can create a risk of fire and/or explosion, leading to loss of life, injury and significant property and environmental damage. Preventing such events or mitigating their effects requires close attention. For advice and guidance on vessel refuelling, please see Marine Notice No. 61 of 2023.

Appliances should be securely fixed to the craft. Where a cooker is mounted on a gimball arrangement, its freedom to move should not be restricted.

A means of exhaust ventilation should be fitted to the space where a LPG appliance is fitted, with suction ducting located at floor level or in bilge areas. Extraction fans should be located external to the space.

### 10.9.1 Smart Weather Buoys

*Pages 167–168*

10.9 Commissioners of Irish Lights (Irish Lights)

10.9.1 Smart Weather Buoys

Smart Buoys currently provide the mariner with traditional visual navigational information as well as internet-accessible environmental information from around the coast of Ireland, including:

Irish Lights is the General Lighthouse Authority for Ireland, its adjacent seas, and islands. Irish Lights is responsible for the provision and maintenance of lighthouses, buoys, beacons, and radio aids to marine navigation (AtoN) to assist the safe and continuous passage of all classes of mariners in general navigation and has a duty of superintendence and management for all local AtoN. Irish Lights is required to inspect Local AtoN and report the result of those inspections to the relevant Government Minister. AtoN cannot be established, disestablished or altered without prior consent from Irish Lights.

▪Sea state (Wave Height, Wave

Period)

▪Weather conditions (Wind Speed,

Wind Direction, Gust Speed, Gust Direction)

▪Water Temperature.

Information and data feedback from the smart weather buoys around the coast are available at MetOcean Charts (irishlights.ie).

All radio navigation systems are susceptible to interference (including jamming) and environmental effects, which can adversely affect their availability. Irish Lights strongly advises that no single Aids to Navigation system should be used in isolation and that users should use all alternative means available to cross check the information received. Further information can be found at www.irishlights.ie.

If you see an Aid to Navigation that is not functioning correctly, please contact the Irish Lights 24 hour emergency number on +353 1 280 1996.

### 11.1.2 Making an urgency broadcast using VHF - PAN-PAN

*Pages 170–172*

CALLSIGN EI __ __ __ __ (Or other identification)

The procedures described in this Chapter do not prevent the use by any vessel, survival craft or person in distress of any means at their disposal to attract attention, make known their position and obtain help.

Emergency Procedures

MAYDAY,

‘NAME OF VESSEL’,

CALLSIGN EI __ __ __ __

STATE THE POSITION OF THE VESSEL.

(If possible give the position from a GPS receiver or bearings from and distance to any known fixed points e.g. “Position one mile South of Fastnet Lighthouse”)

11.1 Procedure for making distress or urgency calls using VHF voice 11.1.1 Making a distress transmission using VHF voice broadcasting – MAYDAY

STATE THE NATURE OF DISTRESS. (e.g. sinking, man overboard, fire on board, …)

STATE THE NUMBER OF PERSONS ABOARD.

STATE ANY OTHER USEFUL INFORMATION.

In cases where there is grave or imminent danger to either the vessel or persons on board, including man overboard situations, then a MAYDAY should be broadcast on Channel 16 with the VHF unit set to high power in the following format, after pressing the VHF Distress Button (if your VHF has a DSC function):

OVER”.

11.1.2 Making an urgency broadcast using VHF - PAN-PAN

In circumstances that are considered to be grave but do not require immediate assistance and where there is no imminent danger to the vessel or persons on board, e.g. mechanical failure, loss of propulsion, a “PAN-PAN” Urgency Broadcast should be made on VHF Channel 16 with the unit set to high power in the following format:

“MAYDAY, MAYDAY, MAYDAY,

This is ‘NAME OF VESSEL’, ‘NAME OF VESSEL’, ‘NAME OF VESSEL’

(State the name of the vessel three times)

“PAN-PAN, PAN-PAN, PAN-PAN,

ALL STATIONS, ALL STATIONS, ALL STATIONS,

THIS IS ‘NAME OF VESSEL’, ‘NAME OF VESSEL’, ‘NAME OF VESSEL’.

(State the vessels name three times)

STATE THE POSITION OF THE VESSEL.

(If possible give the position from a GPS receiver or bearings from and distance to any known fixed points e.g. “Position one mile South of Fastnet Lighthouse”)

STATE THE NATURE OF THE URGENCY.

(e.g. Vessel adrift, mechanical failure)

STATE THE NUMBER OF PERSONS ABOARD.

ASSISTANCE REQUIRED AND ANY FURTHER RELEVANT INFORMATION.

OVER”.

### 11.2.3 Use of Handheld VHF Radios

*Pages 173–173*

11.2 Types of radio distress calls

All information will be displayed on any receiving sets’ display panel. Transmitting and receiving sets will switch to Channel 16 to allow further information to be transmitted, e.g. spoken MAYDAY message, nature of emergency, etc.

The advent of the Global Maritime Distress and Safety System (GMDSS) has brought about a number of changes in the manner and procedure in which distress calls from craft are initiated.

11.2.2 Non-Automated Calling

VHF sets without DSC must rely on the traditional format of broadcast on Channel 16 for making a MAYDAY call. Ensure the set is selected to Channel 16 and that it is transmitting at its full power.

Modern VHF radios are fitted with a Digital Selective Calling (DSC) facility whereby a distress call is activated by pressing a dedicated switch on the radio. This system transmits an all station call on Channel 70.

The MAYDAY broadcast format as described on page 158 should be used.

For non-DSC radios, Channel 16 remains the listening channel for distress calls.

11.2.3 Use of Handheld VHF Radios

Therefore there are two possible scenarios:

Portable VHF radio equipment can be used on small boats particularly where it is impracticable to install a fixed VHF radio. This equipment should be licensed and issued with a radio call sign.

11.2.1 Automated Calling

This only works on DSC radio sets. The operator initiates an all station call by simply uncovering and pushing the red SOS switch on the radio’s panel. This will transmit the Maritime Mobile Service Identity (MMSI) code - a series of 9 digits without any further action required by the crew.

The portable VHF will only be licensed for use on a boat for communications with coast stations, harbour authorities, marinas and other boats - it should not be used on land.

In addition to the MMSI code, if interfaced with a GPS, it can also give a boat’s position and possibly the type of emergency (depending on settings).

### 11.3 Emergency Position Indicating Radio Beacons (EPIRBS)

*Pages 174–174*

11.3 Emergency Position Indicating Radio Beacons (EPIRBS)

![Figure from page 174](assets/p174-f02.webp)

*Figure from page 174*

Emergency Procedures

There is one model of Distress Beacon - the ‘406 MHz’.

406 beacon This is a digital signal and covers the entire globe. 406 beacons have a unique identification code which is part of its signal. When properly registered, the unique code provides information about the boat carrying the beacon. This includes the owner’s emergency contact and the country of registration. The 406 signal may be received within seconds by geostationary satellites.

False alarms from digital 406 MHz beacons can be resolved with a phone call as these devices transmit an identity code that can be crossreferenced with an ownership database.

NOTE: See section 1.2.12.1 for further information regarding EPIRBs and Personal Locator Beacons (PLBs). A PLB is a personal life-saving appliance that can be attached to or form part of your personal flotation device. It operates similarly to an EPIRB by sending a message to the Search and Rescue (SAR) emergency services via satellite.

The registration of an EPIRB may be completed online on the Irish Maritime Radio Licence System (IMRAD). The IMRAD website contains guidance information, Frequently Asked Questions and other useful documents. See www. irishmaritimeradiolicencesystem.gov. ie.

### 11.5 Automatic Identification System (AIS)

*Pages 176–176*

Irish Search and Rescue Region

Emergency Procedures

11.5 Automatic Identification System (AIS)

must also carry AIS. It is important to note that there may still be a large number of vessels not fitted with AIS. It is a useful identification tool but should not be used as a means of collision avoidance as not all vessels are required to carry AIS. Another useful AIS feature is that navigation aids such as buoys and lighthouses are increasingly being fitted with AIS transponders to assist with identification. AIS Search And Rescue Transponders (SART) are also available that can assist in locating a casualty in a Search and Rescue situation.

The Automatic Identification System (AIS) is an automatic tracking system used on ships and by vessel traffic services (VTS) for identifying and locating vessels by electronically exchanging data. AIS has been widely adopted and can be a useful tool on board recreational vessels. It can assist in identifying larger vessels as all passenger ships must have AIS. All ships of 300 gross tonnage and upwards engaged on international voyages, as well as cargo ships of 500 gross tonnage and upwards on domestic voyages,

### 11.6.1 1 Cold Water Shock

*Pages 177–177*

11.6 Survival at sea

11.6.1 Cold Water Shock and Hypothermia

Death by hypothermia or drowning presents the greatest risk to individuals who are forced to abandon their craft or who fall into the water. The ambient sea/water temperature can cause people to very quickly become cold and affect their ability to help themselves once in the water.

When cold water (temperature under 15 degrees) makes contact with your skin, the cold shock response causes an immediate loss of breathing control, possible dizziness and panic. This dramatically increases the risk of sudden drowning even if the water is calm and you know how to swim. For those who survive this, but are unable to get out of the water, progressive body cooling leads to hypothermia and muscle cooling, making swimming more difficult or impossible. Children cool even faster than adults.

![Figure from page 177](assets/p177-f02.webp)

*Figure from page 177*

If you fall into cold water, avoid swimming, stay calm, try to relax and breathe normally. Swimming may give a feeling of warmth but it accelerates muscle cooling. Float or tread water while you catch your breath. To lessen heat escape, keep your legs together and elbows by your side. Follow the “Float to live” safety advice – see page 9. If you are not wearing a Personal Flotation Device or lifejacket, lay your head back with ears submerged. A properly fitted lifejacket will keep your head above water, ensuring you gasp air. Once the cold shock has passed, try to get hold of something that will help you float and get as much of your body out of the water as possible. Once you are calm, call for help.

After boarding a liferaft it is still possible to succumb to hypothermia. Individuals should take the necessary survival precautions.

Survival at sea even for relatively short periods of time is dependent on suitable equipment, adequate preparation and knowledge of survival techniques.

A number of recognised course providers offer a one day Basic Sea Survival Course (see Appendix 9 for details of course providers) covering both the theoretical and practical aspects of sea survival techniques.

### 11.6.1 2 Hypothermia

*Pages 178–178*

11.6.1.1 Cold Water Shock

11.6.1.2 Hypothermia

When you are immersed in water, the skin and limbs cool rapidly. Then the heart, brain and internal organs cool. Hypothermia occurs when deep body temperature drops by at least 2°C. The rate of onset will depend on water temperature and the protective measures you have taken to reduce body cooling. Body build, body fat, fitness level and types of clothing worn all affect the rate of onset.

Emergency Procedures

▪Initial deep gasping

▪Uncontrollable rapid breathing,

with possible dizziness and pins and needles

▪Panic

▪A large increase in both heart rate

and blood pressure

▪Drowning

▪Early dulling of sensation in hands

▪Stroke or heart attack

and impaired muscle function

around the lips

circulation

▪Death

Note: Do not assume a person is dead. They may only be in hibernation.

### 11.6.3 Abandoning Ship

*Pages 181–181*

11.6.3 Abandoning Ship

▪Canopy – protects the crew

from the elements, reduces risk of exposure and improves the chances of the raft avoiding total inversion if capsized by a wave or inflating upside down.

The decision to abandon a ship should only be taken if absolutely necessary. Often a damaged or incapacitated boat will, even in adverse weather conditions, offer greater protection to a crew than entering the water or deploying a liferaft.

▪Inflatable Floor – offers improved

insulation against the cold.

▪Sea Anchor - offers relative

stability to the raft in the sea.

The decision to abandon a ship must be made taking into consideration a number of factors:

▪Boarding Aid – offers essential

assistance to a fully clothed person attempting to enter a liferaft from the water.

▪Condition of the boat, propulsion

and power capabilities.

Liferafts require regular servicing by trained personnel and at intervals laid down by their manufacturer, which should always be adhered to.

▪Internal flooding.

▪Bilge Pumping capacities and

capabilities.

▪Weather conditions.

![Figure from page 181](assets/p181-f02.webp)

*Figure from page 181*

▪Communications with rescue

facilities.

▪Physical condition of crew.

If abandoning a craft to a liferaft, the following points should be adhered to:

▪Be familiar with the correct

method of launching. Read the instructions before departure and ensure other crew are familiar with the location of the liferaft and the means of deployment.

Liferafts should be stowed on board in a location where they can be rapidly deployed. They can be stowed either on deck or in a locker opening directly onto the deck. If stowed on deck, the storage should be able to withstand heavy weather. All liferafts rely on the attachment of their painter to a suitable strong point on board in order to initiate the inflation procedure.

▪For offshore cruising: Category

A and B should have a suitably equipped Grab Bag which can be transferred to the liferaft.

▪Before launching the liferaft, check

the water in the launching area is clear of people and obstructions.

11.6.4 Survival in the water

In the event of a liferaft not being available, the following applies:

▪If at all possible, board the raft

without entering the water in order to reduce the effects of the cold.

▪Ensure additional layers of

clothing are worn prior to entering the water.

▪If it is not possible to board the

▪Wear a hat and if possible cover

liferaft without entering the water, choose a suitable place to leave the boat while taking account of the sea state and drift of the boat. Remember, liferafts can drift much faster than most people can swim.

the extremities of the body, e.g. fingers, toes and face.

▪Do not jump into the sea. Use an

overside ladder if available. Avoid obstructions in the water adjacent to the hull. It may be preferable to abandon from either the bow or stern rather than amidships.

▪Wear additional layers of clothing,

including head gear, as this prevents heat loss from the body. A suitable PFD/lifejacket should be worn at all times.

![Figure from page 182](assets/p182-f02.webp)

*Figure from page 182*

▪Do not remain in the water longer

than is necessary.

Once all crew are in the liferaft:

▪The craft should be cut free.

▪Manoeuvre clear of the craft or

any obstructions.

▪Deploy the sea anchor.

▪Close all entrances to conserve

heat.

▪Issue sea sickness tablets.

▪Post a lookout.

▪Maintain the liferaft - inflate the

floor, bale out any water, check for leaks, ventilate by maintaining a small opening.

▪Avoid unnecessary swimming

in order to conserve energy and body heat.

11.7 Man overboard and recovery procedures 11.7.1 Recovery of man overboard

▪Depending on the type of craft,

exercise the required manoeuvre as described in the following section.

The loss of a person overboard presents a serious challenge to those remaining on board who have to safely position the vessel adjacent to the individual in the water and recover the person back on board. Vessels should have a suitable means of re-boarding fitted such as a boarding ladder.

▪Issue MAYDAY and call the Coast

Guard on VHF Channel 16.

Position the person adjacent to the boat and, if the person is physically capable, re-board by means of a suitable boarding ladder or swimming platform.

The situation can be even more traumatic if the skipper is lost and an inexperienced crew are on board.

On losing a crew member overboard, other crew should undertake the following actions:

![Figure from page 183](assets/p183-f01.webp)

*Figure from page 183*

For an exhausted or injured crewmember, external means of recovery will be required, such as:

▪Dedicated recovery sling under

the arms of the individual, using halyards/winches, boom, etc. to provide leverage.

▪Use of victim’s safety harness or

PFD/lifejacket fitted with integral harness.

On boats with outboard engines, the engine may be used as an impromptu ladder to re-board, provided the engine is shut down.

▪Use of a sail deployed over the

side as a scoop and recovered using the boats running rigging.

Emergency Procedures

▪Use of an inflatable tender,

partially deflated to recover the individual from the sea.

Falling into cold water will result in a gasp reflex causing uncontrolled breathing, panic and cardiac stress. A lifejacket will keep your head above water, ensuring you breathe in air.

▪Launch the liferaft and have the

victim recovered into it.

▪On Inflatables, one tube may be

partially deflated to aid recovery of an injured or unconscious person.

Be aware of the dangers of hypothermia affecting persons who have been immersed in the sea for a period of time. Ensure the casualty is kept dry and warm. Alcohol should not be given to the person. Note the advice in section 11.6.1 of this Code.

Many boats are equipped with a bathing platform at the stern which facilitates recovery of persons from the water.

Emergency Procedures

### 11.7.2 2 Craft with an engine

*Pages 186–187*

11.7.2 Manoeuvring boat to aid recovery of man overboard

11.7.2.2 Craft with an engine

To stay as close to the person in the water as possible:

11.7.2.1 Craft without an engine

A simple way to recover is to:

1. Come up to wind and tack, leaving headsail cleated so that boat stops hove to (yachts).

1. Put the craft into an “apparent” beam reach (burgee across the craft). Allow yourself some sea room to manoeuvre and get yourself organised to recover the person from the water.

2. Throw a heaving line to the person in the water if in range, and haul alongside.

2. Tack and sail on the opposite beam reach (person in water now on weather bow).

3. If not within heaving line range: - start the engine

- lower or furl the headsail

3. Approach on a close reach, easing the sheets in the final stages. Leeway will increase as you slow down - allow for this.

- sheet the main sail amidships.

Ensure there are no lines or sheets lying loose on deck or overside that could foul the propeller.

4. In a larger craft, it is easier to come alongside to windward of the person in the water and make the recovery over the leeward side.

4. Motor to leeward of the person in the water and approach the person head to wind.

5. In a dinghy, come alongside to the leeward of the person in the water and make the recovery by the weather shroud.

should proceed slowly with a good lookout as the casualty will be directly ahead of you.

3. In heavy weather, the reciprocal course may bring the sea astern, in which case a short approach head to sea may be more appropriate once the turn has been completed.

2. If you lose sight of the casualty, due to poor visibility or heavy weather and sea state, the “Williamson Turn” is a good way to get to a reciprocal course which will take you back down your track. From the moment the skipper is aware of a “man overboard” situation, put the helm to starboard and adopt a course of “original course + 60 degrees”. Then put the helm immediately to port until the vessel has completed a turn which brings the compass reading to “original course plus or minus 180 degrees”. 5. The initial approach to the person in the water will vary depending on weather/sea conditions and the type of boat. Let the weather help rather than hinder. Stop upwind and drift down.

▪Use the most substantial and

longest line available to you. Join several together using a bowline if necessary.

▪Use a light heaving line as the first

6. If you are concerned about drifting onto the person in the water, bring your stern into the wind. If you are not confident with your boat handling skills or if it looks likely that the boat could come down on top of the person in the water, throw them the heaving line and pull them alongside to a position that is a safe place for recovery.

▪The boat offering the tow must

take care not to foul its own propeller when transferring a tow line, or come in contact with the disabled boat.

▪A towline can be floated

downstream to a disabled craft using a fender.

▪Both craft should use a towing

bridle to secure the towline. Ensure the load is spread over several cleats or strong points on deck such as winches to distribute the load and allow for efficient steering. 7. Ensure the propeller is not turning when you are alongside the person in the water.

11.8 Emergency towing – receiving or giving a tow

Towing should be undertaken with preparation and care by all parties involved in the operation. If assistance is being offered by rescue boats, always follow the instructions of the coxswain or helm as to how to take the line and secure it on board.

▪Ensure an agreed means of

communications is established, either by VHF or hand signals.

In all other circumstances, the following should be observed:

▪The towing boat should slowly

commence to get underway. Speed should be adjusted to suit the vessel being towed and local sea conditions.

▪All crew working on deck must

wear a suitable PFD/lifejacket.

▪Consider the use of lifelines and

If the towed boat is too far aft, the combination of tug and tow can become quite un-manoeuvrable with the two only wishing to alter course in the direction upon which the towed vessel is made fast.

### 11.9 Helicopter rescue procedures

*Pages 190–190*

11.9 Helicopter rescue procedures

you to steer. Generally boats will maintain a course to give the wind at 30 degree to the Port Bow. The preferred area to conduct winching operations is normally the port quarter. This affords the pilot visual contact with both the boat and the winchman.

In the event of a helicopter rescue situation, the following points should be noted and followed:

▪Prepare well in advance of the

arrival of the helicopter; ensure crew are well briefed on correct procedures.

▪A sailing yacht may be steadier

motor sailing with a reefed sail. It stops the mast swirling around, making it easier for the winchman to land on board. However, every opportunity should be utilised to discuss the best approach with the helicopter crew before the helicopter gets close to the vessel, including consideration of the potential impact of downdraft.

▪Clear all obstructions on deck

prior to the arrival of the helicopter. Ensure there are no items of loose or moveable gear on deck.

▪Be prepared for the helicopter

downdraft which can be strong enough to knock a crew member off their feet or cause a leeway effect on small craft.

▪Due to the risk of static build-up

from a hovering helicopter, follow the pilot’s instructions exactly with regard to the earthing of a static discharge wire prior to placing the winchman on board. The wire is usually dropped into the sea to discharge static prior to commencing the operation.

▪All operations will be directed by

the crew of the helicopter – follow all instructions.

▪Liaise with the helicopter before it

nears the vessel due to the noise of the helicopter and the potential impact of downdraft when the helicopter is operating with or adjacent to the vessel.

▪Under no circumstances should

the winch line be made fast at any time to the boat.

▪Do not be distracted by the

noise of the helicopter overhead. It may be necessary to have a crewmember positioned inside the boat to maintain radio communications with the helicopter due to the excessive noise on the outside decks.

▪On arrival of the winchman on

board, that person will assume command of all subsequent operations. Follow the instruction given at all times.

▪Do not fire parachute flares when

a helicopter is operating in the vicinity.

▪The pilot will give specific

instructions regarding the course and direction he/she may wish

### 11.10 Flares

*Pages 191–191*

11.10 Flares

▪Rocket parachute flares can reach

a height of 300 metres and are used for longer range attention seeking.

Flares are an effective way to signal passing aircraft and nearby boats that a vessel is in trouble and requires assistance.

![Figure from page 191](assets/p191-f02.webp)

*Figure from page 191*

There are three types of flares used as distress signals:

▪Red handheld flares are for night-

time use and can be seen up to 10 km away.

![Figure from page 191](assets/p191-f03.webp)

*Figure from page 191*

Flares will burn for about one minute, so only use when other boats and planes are in the area.

White flares are available for the purpose of attracting attention or marking a position by a boat.

![Figure from page 191](assets/p191-f04.webp)

*Figure from page 191*

▪Orange smoke flares are for day

use only and can be seen up to 4 km away on a clear day. They can be either handheld or buoyant cartridge type.

![Figure from page 191](assets/p191-f05.webp)

*Figure from page 191*

Flares are explosives and should be treated with care. Store in a waterproof container. They should always be within their expiry date.

Everyone on board should know where the flares are stored and how to use them.

11.10.1 Disposal of expired pyrotechnics

leisure market in this country are of SOLAS standard.

Expiry dates are generally 3 years from the date of manufacture. Flares should be replaced prior to the expiry date as the chemical components used in flares degrade over time and variations in temperature and humidity can accelerate this process.

Pyrotechnic expiry dates Flares have clearly marked instructions for use and expiry dates printed on the packaging. The expiry dates printed on pyrotechnics (flares) are determined by the required performance of the distress signals as set by marine approval bodies.

Out of date flares may look acceptable to the naked eye. However:

SOLAS standards are used to regulate the quality and performance of distress signals for use on commercial vessels. Recreational craft are not required by law to carry SOLAS approved flares. However, most products supplied for the

▪they can burn at a lower

brightness (candela),

▪the colour of the flare can fade,

▪the burn time can lengthen which

could be a fire hazard,

### 11.11 Mobile Devices

*Pages 193–268*

▪for rockets, the ejection height

▪Misfired or faulty pyrotechnics

and flight stability may be affected,

should not be returned with TEPs. Where pyrotechnics are suspected of being in a dangerous condition, the Gardaí should be notified.

▪red flares can fade and therefore

may not be recognised as a distress signal.

▪TEPs should be contained in a

paper or plastic bag which in turn should be stored in a strong cardboard box.

Expiry dates are set to ensure that pyrotechnics will still perform to the stringent specified quality standards at the end of their official lifetime. The life of the product allows for a performance safety margin so that even if storage conditions are not optimum, the distress signals will still meet the approval bodies’ performance specifications.

▪When transporting TEPs,

packages must be securely stowed to prevent movement during transport. No matches or other goods should be carried in the vehicle.

▪There should be no smoking when

handling or transporting TEPs.

In date flares should be stored on board in a waterproof, buoyant container.

Any queries concerning TEPs should be addressed to the Marine Survey Office at email MSO@transport.gov.ie.

Safe disposal

Out of date flares must be disposed of carefully. Marine Notices are issued from time to time in relation to the safe disposal of time expired pyrotechnics (TEPs), for example Marine Notice No. 13 of 2000 as amended in September 2022. Individuals or organisations may bring TEPSs to certain chandlers, as listed in the Marine Notice, noting the following points:

11.11 Mobile Devices

A mobile phone should not be relied on as the primary method of contacting the emergency services. Having said that, it is recommended that all vessels operating on inland lakes and waterways as well as in coastal waters carry a fully charged mobile phone contained in a watertight storage bag or casing. The mobile phone should at all times be fully charged and the signal strength and the charge indicator of the mobile phone should be regularly checked while the vessel is under way.

▪Expired pyrotechnics should

be handled with due care and diligence. Avoid any action that might cause fire or explosion.

While the complete rulebook forms a large and detailed publication, there are a number of condensed versions available specifically tailored for the recreational boat user. Recreational craft operators should familiarise themselves with some of the rules and regulations so there will be no hesitation on their part, when a possible risk of collision arises.

The International Regulations for Preventing Collisions at Sea (COLREGs)

way to sail. But sailors must be reasonable - don’t expect large, less manoeuvrable vessels under power to give way.

The International Regulations for Preventing Collisions at Sea, COLREGs, govern the interaction of vessels on the water, and apply to all recreational craft at sea and on waters navigable by sea-going vessels. The Regulations also apply to sections of our inland waterways.

▪All small craft should give large

vessels a wide berth.

▪In narrow channels, keep to the

right (starboard). If plenty of distance separates two passing boats, there is no need to deliberately alter course to pass to the right of the other boat.
